Since any quantum state can be reconstructed from its characteristic function via the Fourier-Weyl relation in (
83
), it follows that any Gaussian state is uniquely identified by its first moment and covariance matrix.

An important example of Gaussian state is the so-called
thermal state
. Given
{N_{s}}\geq 0
, the thermal state
\tau_{N_{s}}
is defined as

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\tau_{{N_{s}}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{{N_{s}}+1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{{N_{s}}}{{N_{s}}+1}\right)^{n}\ket{n}\!\!\bra{n}\,.

(92)

Note that the mean photon number of
\tau_{{N_{s}}}
is given by
\Tr[a^{\dagger}a\,\tau_{{N_{s}}}]={N_{s}}
. The first moment and the covariance matrix of the thermal state are given by:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\mathbf{m}(\tau_{{N_{s}}})

\displaystyle=(0,0)^{\intercal}\,,

(93)

\displaystyle V(\tau_{{N_{s}}})

\displaystyle=(2{N_{s}}+1)\mathbb{1}_{2}\,.

Note that for
{N_{s}}=0
, the thermal state coincides with the vacuum state
\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}
. Another important example of Gaussian state is the so-called
\emph{two-modesqueezedvacuumstate}
. Given
N_{s}\geq 0
, the two-mode squeezed vacuum state is a two-mode state defined as

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\ket{\psi_{{N_{s}}}}_{AB}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{{N_{s}}+1}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{{N_{s}}}{{N_{s}}+1}\right)^{n/2}\ket{n}_{A}\otimes\ket{n}_{B}\,.

(94)

The first moment and covariance matrix of the two-mode squeezed vacuum are given by
[
1
]
:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\mathbf{m}(\tau_{{N_{s}}})

\displaystyle=(0,0,0,0)^{\intercal}\,,

(95)

\displaystyle V(\ket{\Psi_{N_{s}}}\!\!\bra{\Psi_{N_{s}}})

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{matrix}(2N_{s}+1)\mathbb{1}_{2}&2\sqrt{N_{s}(N_{s}+1)}\sigma_{z}\\
2\sqrt{N_{s}(N_{s}+1)}\sigma_{z}&(2N_{s}+1)\mathbb{1}_{2}\end{matrix}\right)\,,

where
\sigma_{z}\coloneqq\left(\begin{matrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{matrix}\right)
. Note that the two-mode squeezed vacuum state
\ket{\psi_{{N_{s}}}}
is a purification of the thermal state
\tau_{N_{s}}
, that is
\Tr_{B}\ket{\psi_{{N_{s}}}}\!\!\bra{\psi_{{N_{s}}}}_{AB}=\tau_{N_{s}}
.

The von Neumann entropy of a thermal state
\tau_{N_{s}}
is given by
[
1
]

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptS(\tau_{N_{s}})=h(N_{s})\,,

(96)

where
h(x)\coloneqq(x+1)\log_{2}(x+1)-x\log_{2}x\,
. As proved in
[
49
]
, the
entropy variance

[
49
]
of a thermal state
\tau_{N_{s}}
reads

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Tr[\tau_{N_{s}}(\log_{2}\tau_{N_{s}})^{2}]-\Tr[\tau_{N_{s}}\log_{2}\tau_{N_{s}}]^{2}

\displaystyle=N_{s}(N_{s}+1)\log_{2}\left(1+\frac{1}{N_{s}}\right)^{2}\,.

(97)

A.3.3
Gaussian unitaries

A
symplectic matrix

S\in\mathbb{R}^{2n,2n}
is a matrix satisfying
S\Omega S^{\intercal}=\Omega
. Given a symplectic matrix
S\in\mathbb{R}^{2n,2n}
, one can define a suitable
n
-mode unitary
U_{S}
— dubbed
symplectic unitary
— such that
[
1
]

U_{S}^{\dagger}\hat{R}_{k}U_{S}=\sum^{2n}_{k,l}S_{k,l}\hat{R}_{l}\qquad\forall k\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}\,,

(98)

In a more compact form, this can be written as
U_{S}^{\dagger}\hat{\mathbf{R}}U_{S}=S\hat{\mathbf{R}}
. In particular, a symplectic unitary transforms the first moment and the covariance matrix as follows:

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}(U_{S}\rho U_{S}^{\dagger})=S\mathbf{m}(\rho)\,,

(99)

\displaystyle V(U_{S}\rho U_{S}^{\dagger})=SV\!(\rho)S^{\intercal}\,.

Similarly, the displacement operator transforms the quadrature vector as

\displaystyle\hat{D}_{\mathbf{r}}\hat{\mathbf{R}}\hat{D}_{\mathbf{r}}^{\dagger}=\hat{\mathbf{R}}+\mathbf{r}\mathbb{\hat{1}},

(100)

and, in particular, a displacement operator transforms the first moment and the covariance matrix as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{m}\!\left(\hat{D}_{\mathbf{r}}\rho\hat{D}_{\mathbf{r}}^{\dagger}\right)=\mathbf{m}(\rho)+\mathbf{r}\,,

(101)

\displaystyle V\!\left(\hat{D}_{\mathbf{r}}\rho\hat{D}_{\mathbf{r}}^{\dagger}\right)=V\!(\rho)\,.

Definition 29

((Gaussian unitary))
.

A Gaussian unitary is a unitary given by the product of a displacement operator and a symplectic unitary.

Any Gaussian unitary maps the set of Gaussian states into itself
[
1
]
.

A.3.4
Beam splitter unitary

An important example of Gaussian unitary is the so-called
beam splitter unitary
.

Definition 30

((Beam splitter unitary))
.

Let
S
and
E
be two single-mode systems. Let
a
and
b
be the annihilation operators of
S
and
E
, respectively. For all
\lambda\in[0,1]
, the beam splitter unitary of transmissivity
\lambda
is defined by

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptU_{\lambda}^{SE}\coloneqq\exp\!\left[\arccos\sqrt{\lambda}\left(a^{\dagger}b-a\,b^{\dagger}\right)\right]\,.

(102)

The symplectic matrix
S_{\lambda}
associated with the beam splitter unitary
U^{SE}_{\lambda}
satisfying the relation
(U^{SE}_{\lambda})^{\dagger}\hat{\mathbf{R}}\,U^{SE}_{\lambda}=S_{\lambda}\hat{\mathbf{R}}
is given by
[
1
]

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptS_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\left(\begin{matrix}\sqrt{\lambda}\,\mathbb{1}_{2}&\sqrt{1-\lambda}\,\mathbb{1}_{2}\\
-\sqrt{1-\lambda}\,\mathbb{1}_{2}&\sqrt{\lambda}\,\mathbb{1}_{2}\end{matrix}\right)\,.

(103)

The beam splitter unitary
U^{SE}_{\lambda}
acts acts on a Fock state
\ket{i}_{S}\otimes\ket{j}_{E}
as (see e.g.
[
74
]
)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptU_{\lambda}\ket{i}_{S}\otimes\ket{j}_{E}=\sum_{m=0}^{i+j}c_{m}^{(i,j)}\ket{i+j-m}_{S}\otimes\ket{m}_{E}\,,

(104)

where

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptc_{m}^{(i,j)}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{i!j!}}\sum_{k=\max(0,m-j)}^{\min(i,m)}(-1)^{k}\sqrt{m!(i+j-m)!}\binom{i}{k}\binom{j}{m-k}\lambda^{\frac{i+m-2k}{2}}(1-\lambda)^{\frac{j+2k-m}{2}}\,.

(105)

In particular, it holds that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptU_{\lambda}^{SE}\ket{n}_{S}\otimes\ket{0}_{E}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{n}(-1)^{l}\sqrt{\binom{n}{l}}\lambda^{\frac{n-l}{2}}(1-\lambda)^{\frac{l}{2}}\ket{n-l}_{S}\otimes\ket{l}_{E}\,,

(106)

\displaystyle U_{\lambda}^{SE}\ket{0}_{S}\otimes\ket{n}_{E}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{n}\sqrt{\binom{n}{l}}(1-\lambda)^{\frac{l}{2}}\lambda^{\frac{n-l}{2}}\ket{l}_{S}\otimes\ket{n-l}_{E}\,.

A.3.5
Two-mode squeezing unitary

Another important example of Gaussian unitary is the so-called
two-mode squeezing unitary
.

Definition 31

((Two-mode squeezing unitary))
.

Let
S
and
E
be two single-mode systems. Let
a
and
b
be the annihilation operators of
S
and
E
, respectively. For all
g\geq 1
, the two-mode squeezing unitary of parameter
g
is defined by

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptU_{g}^{SE}\coloneqq\exp\left[\arccosh\sqrt{g}\left(a^{\dagger}b^{\dagger}-a\,b\right)\right]\,.

(107)

The symplectic matrix
S_{g}
associated with the two-mode squeezing unitary
U^{SE}_{g}
satisfying the relation
(U^{SE}_{g})^{\dagger}\hat{\mathbf{R}}\,U^{SE}_{g}=S_{g}\hat{\mathbf{R}}
is given by
[
1
]

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptS_{g}\coloneqq\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt{g}\,\mathbb{1}_{2}\,&\,\sqrt{g-1}\,\sigma_{z}\\
\sqrt{g-1}\,\sigma_{z}\,&\,\sqrt{g}\,\mathbb{1}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(108)

A.3.6
Williamson decomposition

The covariance matrix
V\!(\rho)
of an
n
-mode state
\rho
satisfies the so-called
Williamson decomposition

[
1
]
: there exist
n
real numbers
d_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{n}\geq 1
, known as the
symplectic eigenvalues
of
V\!(\rho)
, and a symplectic matrix
S
such that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptV\!(\rho)=S\,\operatorname{diag}\left(d_{1},d_{1},d_{2},d_{2},\ldots,d_{n},d_{n}\right)\,S^{\intercal}\,,\,.

(109)

Moreover, if
\rho
is a Gaussian state, one can show that the above decomposition of the covariance matrix implies the following decomposition of
\rho
, known as
normal decomposition
:
[
1
]

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\rho=\hat{D}_{\mathbf{m}(\rho)}U_{S}\left(\tau_{\nu_{1}}\otimes\tau_{\nu_{2}}\otimes\ldots\tau_{\nu_{n}}\right)U_{S}^{\dagger}\hat{D}_{\mathbf{m}(\rho)}^{\dagger}\,,

(110)

where
\{\tau_{\nu_{k}}\}^{n}_{k=1}
are thermal states defined in (
92
), and the mean photon numbers
\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{n}
are defined in terms of the symplectic eigenvalues of
V\!(\rho)
as
\nu_{i}\coloneqq\frac{d_{i}-1}{2}
.

The above decomposition allows us to easily show the following useful lemma.

Lemma 32
.

For any Gaussian state
\rho
, the quantity
\Tr\sqrt{\rho}
is finite.

Proof.

Thanks to the decomposition of normal decomposition in (
110
), any Gaussian state
\rho
is unitarily equivalent to a tensor product of thermal states. Hence, the trace of the square root of a Gaussian state is equal to the product of traces of the square root of thermal states. Moreover, the trace of the square root of a thermal state
\tau_{N}
is finite, as

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\sqrt{\tau_{{N}}}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{{N}+1}}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{{N}}{{N}+1}\right)^{n/2}\ket{n}\!\!\bra{n}\,.

(111)

and hence

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Tr[\sqrt{\tau_{N}}]=\frac{1}{N+1}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{N}{N+1}\right)^{n/2}<\infty\,.

(112)

This concludes the proof.
∎

A.3.7
Gaussian channels

Definition 33

((Gaussian channel))
.

A Gaussian channel is a quantum channel that maps the set of Gaussian states into itself.

An
n
-mode Gaussian channel
is a Gaussian channel from the set of
n
-mode states into itself. Similarly, a
single-mode Gaussian channel
is a Gaussian channel from the set of single-mode states into itself. It is worth to mention that any Gaussian channel can be written in Stinespring representation in terms of a Gaussian unitary and an environmental vacuum state
[
1
]
.

An important example of single-mode Gaussian channel is the so-called
pure loss channel
, which models the phenomenon of photon loss in optical fibres and free-space links. The pure loss channel is mathematically defined in the forthcoming Definition
34
, with a pictorial representation provided in Fig.
2
.

Definition 34

((Pure loss channel))
.

Let
S
and
E
be two single-mode systems. The pure loss channel of transmissivity
\lambda
is a quantum channel
\pazocal{E}_{\lambda}:S\to S
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\pazocal{E}_{\lambda}(\Theta)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Tr_{E}\left[U_{\lambda}^{SE}\big(\Theta_{S}\otimes\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{E}\big)(U_{\lambda}^{SE})^{\dagger}\right]

(113)

for any linear operator
\Theta
on
S
, where
U_{\lambda}^{SE}
denotes the beam splitter unitary of transmissivity
\lambda
and
\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{E}
denotes the vacuum state of
E
.

Figure 2:
Pictorial representation of the pure loss channel. The pure loss channel
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}
of transmissivity
\lambda
acts on the input state
\rho
by mixing it in a beam splitter (i.e. a semi-reflective mirror) of transmissivity
\lambda
with an environment initialised in the vacuum
\ket{0}_{E}
. The pure loss channel is noiseless for
\lambda=1
(it equals the identity channel), while it is completely noisy for
\lambda=0
(it maps any input state into the vacuum).

Another notable example of a single-mode Gaussian channel is the
pure amplifier channel
, which serves as a model for phenomena such as spontaneous parametric down-conversion
[
56
]
, the dynamical Casimir effect in superconducting circuits
[
57
]
, the Unruh effect
[
58
]
, and Hawking radiation
[
59
]
.

Definition 35

((Pure amplifier channel))
.

Let
S
and
E
be two single-mode systems. The pure amplifier channel of gain
g
is a quantum channel
\Phi_{g}:S\to S
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Phi_{g}(\Theta)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Tr_{E}\left[U_{g}^{SE}\big(\Theta_{S}\otimes\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{E}\big)(U_{g}^{SE})^{\dagger}\right]

(114)

for any linear operator
\Theta
on
S
, where
U_{g}^{SE}
denotes the two-mode squeezing unitary of parameter
g
and
\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{E}
denotes the vacuum state of
E
.

A.3.8
Capacities of the pure loss channel and pure amplifier channel

For all
\lambda\in[0,1]
the quantum capacity
Q(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda})

[
12
,
14
,
60
]
, the two-way quantum capacity
Q_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda})

[
25
]
, and the secret-key capacity
K(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda})

[
25
]
of the pure loss channel
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}
are given by:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptQ(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}\right)&\text{if $\lambda\in(\frac{1}{2},1]$ ,}\\
0&\text{if $\lambda\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]$ ,}\end{cases}

(115)

\displaystyle Q_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=K(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda})=\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\right)\,.

Hence, the quantum capacity vanishes for
\lambda\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]
, while the two-way quantum capacity and secret-key capacity are always larger than zero for all
\lambda\in(0,1]
.

Moreover, for all
\Phi_{g}\geq 1
the quantum capacity
Q(\Phi_{g})

[
12
,
14
,
60
]
, the two-way quantum capacity
Q_{2}(\Phi_{g})

[
25
]
, and the secret-key capacity
K(\Phi_{g})

[
25
]
of the pure amplifier channel
\Phi_{g}
are all equal to each other and are given by:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptQ(\Phi_{g})=Q_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=K(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda})=\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{g}{g-1}\right)\,.

(116)

The above capacities can be calculated via coherent information quantities. Namely, for
\lambda\in[\frac{1}{2},1]
the quantum capacity
Q(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda})
of the pure loss channel is given by
[
12
,
14
,
60
]
:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptQ(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda})=\lim\limits_{N_{s}\rightarrow\infty}I_{c}(A\,\rangle\,B)_{\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}(\ket{\Psi_{N_{s}}}\!\!\bra{\Psi_{N_{s}}}_{AB})}=\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{\lambda}{1-\lambda}\right)\,,

(117)

where
\ket{\Psi_{N_{s}}}\!\!\bra{\Psi_{N_{s}}}_{AB}
denotes the two-mode squeezed vacuum state defined in Eq. (
94
) and
I_{c}(A\,\rangle\,B)
denotes the coherent information defined in Definition
20
. Moreover, the two-way quantum capacity and the secret-key capacity are given by
[
25
]
:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptQ_{2}(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda})=K(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda})=\lim\limits_{N_{s}\rightarrow\infty}I_{c}(B\,\rangle\,A)_{\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}(\ket{\Psi_{N_{s}}}\!\!\bra{\Psi_{N_{s}}}_{AB})}=\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\right)\,.

(118)

Similarly, the capacities of the pure amplifier channel can be calculated via coherent information quantities as

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptQ_{2}(\Phi_{g})=Q_{2}(\Phi_{g})=K(\Phi_{g})=\lim\limits_{N_{s}\rightarrow\infty}I_{c}(A\,\rangle\,B)_{\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Phi_{g}(\ket{\Psi_{N_{s}}}\!\!\bra{\Psi_{N_{s}}}_{AB})}=\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{g}{g-1}\right)\,.

(119)

An upper bound on the
n
-shot capacities of the pure loss channel and of the pure amplifier channel is given by
[
29
]

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptQ_{2}^{(\varepsilon,n)}(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\leq K^{(\varepsilon,n)}(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda})\leq n\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{1}{1-\lambda}\right)+\log_{2}6+2\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{1+\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\right)\,,

(120)

\displaystyle Q_{2}^{(\varepsilon,n)}(\Phi_{g})

\displaystyle\leq K^{(\varepsilon,n)}(\Phi_{g})\leq n\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{g}{g-1}\right)+\log_{2}6+2\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{1+\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}\right)\,,

while a lower bound is proved in the present paper; specifically in Section
D
for the pure loss channel and in Section
E
for the pure amplifier channel.

A.3.9
Energy-constrained capacities

In practise, a quantum communication protocol can not exploit
infinite
energy. Instead, all optical input signals used in a communication protocol have a bounded energy (bounded by e.g. the energy of the Sun, or the energy budget available in lab). It is therefore important to consider only those quantum communication protocols that exploit input states satisfying a suitable
energy constraint
. Specifically, in the continuous-variable setting, it is common to consider the so-called
energy-constrained capacities

[
26
,
16
]
. Given a positive number
N_{s}
(representing the energy budget per input signal), the energy-constrained capacities are defined in the same way as the unconstrained capacities, apart from the fact that the optimisation is performed over those protocols such that the average expected value of the photon number operator
a^{\dagger}a
on all input signals is required to be at most
N_{s}
. The energy-constrained quantum capacities of a quantum channel
\Phi
are denoted as
Q(\Phi,N_{s})
,
Q_{2}(\Phi,N_{s})
, and
K(\Phi,N_{s})
, while the
n
-shot energy-constrained capacities are denoted as
Q^{(\varepsilon,n)}(\Phi,N_{s})
,
Q_{2}^{(\varepsilon,n)}(\Phi,N_{s})
, and
K^{(\varepsilon,n)}(\Phi,N_{s})
.

The energy-constrained quantum capacity of the pure loss channel
\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}
and of the pure amplifier channel
\Phi_{g}
have been determined exactly
[
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
,
19
]
and they are given by the coherent information of the bipartite state obtained by sending an half of the two-mode squeezed vacuum state
\ket{\Psi_{N_{s}}}\!\!\bra{\Psi_{N_{s}}}
with local energy
N_{s}
into the channel:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptQ\left(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda},N_{s}\right)

\displaystyle=\max\{0,I_{c}(A\,\rangle\,B)_{\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}(\ket{\Psi_{N_{s}}}\!\!\bra{\Psi_{N_{s}}}_{AB})}\}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}h(\lambda N_{s})-h((1-\lambda)N_{s})&\text{if $\lambda\in(\frac{1}{2},1]$ ,}\\
0&\text{if $\lambda\in[0,\frac{1}{2}]$ ,}\end{cases}

(121)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptQ\left(\Phi_{g},N_{s}\right)

\displaystyle=I_{c}(A\,\rangle\,B)_{\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Phi_{g}(\ket{\Psi_{N_{s}}}\!\!\bra{\Psi_{N_{s}}}_{AB})}=h\!\left(gN_{s}+g-1\right)-h\!\left((g-1)(N_{s}+1)\right)\,,

(122)

where
h(x)\coloneqq(x+1)\log_{2}(x+1)-x\log_{2}x
.

The energy-constrained two-way quantum and secret-key capacities of the pure loss channel and pure amplifier channel have not yet been determined. However, a lower bound is given by
[
31
]
:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptK\left(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda},N_{s}\right)

\displaystyle\geq Q_{2}\left(\mathcal{E}_{\lambda},N_{s}\right)\geq I_{c}(B\,\rangle\,A)_{\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{\lambda}(\ket{\Psi_{N_{s}}}\!\!\bra{\Psi_{N_{s}}}_{AB})}=h(N_{s})-h\!\left((1-\lambda)N_{s}\right)\,,

(123)

\displaystyle K\left(\Phi_{g},N_{s}\right)

\displaystyle\geq Q_{2}\left(\Phi_{g},N_{s}\right)\geq Q\left(\Phi_{g},N_{s}\right)=I_{c}(A\,\rangle\,B)_{\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Phi_{g}(\ket{\Psi_{N_{s}}}\!\!\bra{\Psi_{N_{s}}}_{AB})}=h\!\left(gN_{s}+g-1\right)-h\!\left((g-1)(N_{s}+1)\right)\,.

In this work, we will establish a lower bound on the energy-constrained
n
-shot capacities of the pure loss channel and pure amplifier channel.

Appendix B
Tail bounds on Gaussian states

In this section, we solve the following simple-looking problems:

•

What is the probability that a fixed Gaussian state has more than
M
photons? In other words, given a Gaussian state
\rho
, how to estimate the probability that the outcome of the photon number measurement on
\rho
is larger than
M
? We answer this question in subsection (
B.1
). In particular, we show that such a probability converges to zero exponentially fast in
M
, with exponential lifetime given by the mean photon number of the state.

•

What is the trace-distance error in approximating a Gaussian state with its finite-dimensional approximation consisting of at most
M
photons? Equivalently, given a Gaussian state
\rho
, how to estimate the trace distance between
\rho
and its projection
\rho_{M}
onto the finite-dimensional subspace with fixed maximum number of photons
M
? We answer this question in subsection (
B.2
). Specifically, we prove that such an error converges to zero exponentially fast in
M
, with exponential lifetime given by the mean photon number of the state.

To solve both problems, we determine explicit bounds which can be written only in terms of the first moment and covariance matrix of the state. We believe that these bounds provide technical tools of independent interest, which can be applied in all areas of quantum information with continuous variable systems. Indeed, by exploiting these tools, we design an efficient algorithm to compute the trace distance between two Gaussian states up to precision
\varepsilon
in trace distance, which is also of independent interest. Finally, these new tools will turn out to be crucial in our analysis of one-shot bosonic quantum communication in Section
C
.

B.1
Probability that a fixed Gaussian state has more than
M
photons

In this subsection, we solve the following problem. Given a Gaussian state
\rho
specified by its first moment
m
and covariance matrix
V
, how to estimate the probability
P_{>M}
that the outcome of the photon number measurement on
\rho
is larger than
M
, as a function of
m
,
V
, and
M
? Estimating such a probability
P_{>M}
is important, as the photon number measurement is a key element in the design of quantum technologies based on continuous-variable systems
[
1
]
.

By Born’s rule, such a probability
P_{>M}
is given by

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptP_{>M}\coloneqq\Tr[(\mathbb{1}-\Pi_{M})\rho]\,,

(124)

where
\Pi_{M}
is the projector onto the Hilbert space spanned by all the
n
-mode Fock states with photon number less or equal to
M
:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Pi_{M}\coloneqq\sum_{\textbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}:\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}\leq M}\ket{\textbf{k}}\!\!\bra{\textbf{k}}\,,

(125)

with
\ket{\textbf{k}}=\ket{k_{1}}\otimes\ket{k_{2}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\ket{k_{n}}
being the
n
-mode Fock state. The following theorem allows us to upper bound the probability
P_{>M}
, establishing that it converges to zero exponentially fast in
M
.

Theorem 36

((Probability that a fixed Gaussian state has more than
M
photons))
.

Let
\rho
be an
n
-mode Gaussian state with first moment
m
and covariance matrix
V
. The probability
P_{>M}
that the outcome of the photon number measurement on
\rho
is larger than
M
converges to zero exponentially fast in
M
. Specifically, it can be upper bounded as

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptP_{>M}\leq\alpha\,e^{-M/(4N+2)}\,,

(126)

where
N
denotes the mean photon number given by
N=\frac{\Tr[V-\mathbb{1}]}{4}+\frac{\|\textbf{m}\|_{2}^{2}}{2}
, while
\alpha
denotes a finite quantity defined in terms of the first moment
m
and covariance matrix
V
as

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\alpha

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{e^{\textbf{m}^{\intercal}((8N+4)\mathbb{1}-V)^{-1}\textbf{m}}}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{(8N+4)\mathbb{1}-V}{8N+3}\right]}}\leq 2^{n}e^{1/2}\,.

(127)

A strictly tighter bound can be obtained in terms of the following one-parameter optimisation problem:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptP_{>M}\leq\inf_{x>\|V\|_{\infty}}\frac{e^{\textbf{m}^{\intercal}(x\mathbb{1}-V)^{-1}\textbf{m}}}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{x\mathbb{1}-V}{x-1}\right]}}\,e^{-2\,\mathrm{arccoth}(x)\,M}\,,

(128)

where
\|V\|_{\infty}
denotes the operator norm of
V
.

Before proving Theorem
36
, we need to establish some preliminary lemmas.
Let us start with the following.

Lemma 37
.

The operator norm of the covariance matrix of an
n
-mode quantum state
\rho
can be upper bounded in terms of its mean photon number
N\coloneqq\Tr[\hat{N}_{n}\rho]
as

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\|V(\rho)\|_{\infty}\leq 1+2N+2\sqrt{N^{2}+N}\,,

(129)

where
\hat{N}_{n}
denotes the total photon number operator.

Proof.

Note that the spectrum of any covariance matrix
V(\rho)
is of the form
\{w_{1},\frac{c_{1}}{w_{1}},\ldots,w_{n},\frac{c_{n}}{w_{n}}\}
, where
w_{1},w_{2},\ldots,w_{n}\geq 1
and
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\geq 1

[
75
]
. To prove this simple claim, note that Williamson’s decomposition
[
1
]
establishes that there exists a symplectic matrix
S
and a diagonal matrix
D\geq\mathbb{1}
such that
V(\rho)=SDS^{\intercal}
. In particular, it holds that
V(\rho)\geq SS^{\intercal}
. By Euler decomposition
[
1
]
, the spectrum of
SS^{\intercal}
is of the form
\{z_{1},\frac{1}{z_{1}},\ldots,z_{n},\frac{1}{z_{n}}\}
, where
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n}\geq 1
. Since
V(\rho)\geq SS^{\intercal}
, Weyl’s monotonicity theorem
[
76
]
implies the claim.

With this claim at hand, note that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Tr V(\rho)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(w_{i}+\frac{c_{i}}{w_{i}}\right)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(w_{i}+\frac{1}{w_{i}}\right)=\|V(\rho)\|_{\infty}+\frac{1}{\|V(\rho)\|_{\infty}}+\sum_{i=2}^{n}\left(w_{i}+\frac{1}{w_{i}}\right)\geq\frac{1}{\|V(\rho)\|_{\infty}}+\|V(\rho)\|_{\infty}+2n-2\,\,,

(130)

where the second equality follows from the fact that
w_{1}
is the largest eigenvalue of the positive matrix
V(\rho)
, and thus it equals the operator norm of
V(\rho)
, while in the last inequality we exploited that
\min_{x\geq 1}(x+\frac{1}{x})=2
.
In particular, by using that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Tr V=2\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\Tr[\hat{R}_{i}^{2}\rho]-2\sum_{i=1}^{2n}[m_{i}(\rho)]^{2}\leq 2\sum_{i=1}^{2n}\Tr[\hat{R}_{i}^{2}\rho]=4\Tr[\hat{N}_{n}\rho]+2n=4N+2n\,,

(131)

we obtain that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\frac{1}{\|V(\rho)\|_{\infty}}+\|V(\rho)\|_{\infty}\leq 4N+2\,.

(132)

Since
\|V(\rho)\|_{\infty}\geq 1
, by solving the above inequality with respect to
\|V(\rho)\|_{\infty}
, we conclude that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\|V(\rho)\|_{\infty}\leq 1+2N+2\sqrt{N^{2}+N}\,.

(133)

∎

For the rest of this subsection, we will use the following notation. First, let us recall that the Gaussian state
\rho_{\textbf{m},V}
with first moment
m
and covariance matrix
V
can be expressed as
[
1
]

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\rho_{\textbf{m},V}=D_{\textbf{m}}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\textbf{R}}^{\intercal}H_{V}\textbf{R}}}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{V+i\Omega}{2}\right]}}D_{\textbf{m}}^{\dagger}\,,

(134)

where
D_{\textbf{m}}
denotes the displacement operator and

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptH_{V}\coloneqq 2i\Omega\mathrm{arccoth}(Vi\Omega)\,.

(135)

For any
\alpha>0
, let us denote as
\rho_{\textbf{m},V}^{(\alpha)}
the Gaussian state proportional to
(\rho_{\textbf{m},V})^{\alpha}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\rho_{\textbf{m},V}^{(\alpha)}\coloneqq\frac{(\rho_{\textbf{m},V})^{\alpha}}{\Tr[(\rho_{\textbf{m},V})^{\alpha}]}=D_{\textbf{m}}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\textbf{R}}^{\intercal}(\alpha H_{V})\textbf{R}}}{\Tr\left[e^{-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\textbf{R}}^{\intercal}(\alpha H_{V})\textbf{R}}\right]}D_{\textbf{m}}^{\dagger}\,.

(136)

Moreover, given a covariance matrix
V
, let
V^{(\alpha)}
be the covariance matrix of the Gaussian state
\rho_{\textbf{m},V}^{(\alpha)}
. In other words, we can write

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\rho_{\textbf{m},V}^{(\alpha)}=\rho_{\textbf{m},V^{(\alpha)}}\,.

(137)

We are now ready to state the following lemma
[
77
, Corollary 20]
.

Lemma 38

((Overlap between the square of a Gaussian state and the inverse of a Gaussian state
[
77
]
))
.

Let
\rho_{\textbf{m},V}
and
\rho_{\textbf{t},W}
be Gaussian states with first moments
\textbf{m},\textbf{t}
, respectively, and covariance matrices
V,W
, respectively. Let
V^{(2)}
as defined in (
137
). If
W-V^{(2)}>0
, then it holds that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Tr[\rho_{\textbf{m},V}^{2}\rho_{\textbf{t},W}^{-1}]=\frac{\det\!\left[\frac{W+i\Omega}{2}\right]}{\det\!\left[\frac{V+i\Omega}{2}\right]}\frac{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{V^{(2)}+i\Omega}{2}\right]}}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{W-V^{(2)}}{2}\right]}}e^{(\textbf{m}-\textbf{t})^{\intercal}(W-V^{(2)})^{-1}(\textbf{m}-\textbf{t})}\,.

(138)

With this lemma at hand, we can prove the following result.

Lemma 39

((Overlap between a Gaussian state and the inverse of a Gaussian state))
.

Let
\rho_{\textbf{m},V}
and
\rho_{\textbf{t},W}
be Gaussian states with first moments
\textbf{m},\textbf{t}
, respectively, and covariance matrices
V,W
, respectively. If
W-V>0
, then it holds that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Tr\!\left[\rho_{\textbf{m},V}(\rho_{\textbf{t},W})^{-1}\right]=\frac{\det\!\left[\frac{W+i\Omega}{2}\right]}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{W-V}{2}\right]}}e^{(\textbf{m}-\textbf{t})^{\intercal}(W-V)^{-1}(\textbf{m}-\textbf{t})}\,.

(139)

Proof.

The crux of the proof is to exploit the known formula for the overlap between the square of a Gaussian state and the inverse of a Gaussian state reported in Lemma
38
. First, let us note that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\rho_{\textbf{m},V}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(i)}}}}{{=}}D_{\textbf{m}}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\textbf{R}}^{\intercal}H_{V}\textbf{R}}}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{V+i\Omega}{2}\right]}}D_{\textbf{m}}^{\dagger}

(140)

\displaystyle=\frac{\det\!\left[\frac{V^{(1/2)}+i\Omega}{2}\right]}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{V+i\Omega}{2}\right]}}\left(D_{\textbf{m}}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\textbf{R}}^{\intercal}(H_{V}/2)\textbf{R}}}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{V^{(1/2)}+i\Omega}{2}\right]}}D_{\textbf{m}}^{\dagger}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(ii)}}}}{{=}}\frac{\det\!\left[\frac{V^{(1/2)}+i\Omega}{2}\right]}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{V+i\Omega}{2}\right]}}\left(D_{\textbf{m}}\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\textbf{R}}^{\intercal}(H_{V}/2)\textbf{R}}}{\Tr\left[e^{-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\textbf{R}}^{\intercal}(H_{V}/2)\textbf{R}}\right]}D_{\textbf{m}}^{\dagger}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(iii)}}}}{{=}}\frac{\det\!\left[\frac{V^{(1/2)}+i\Omega}{2}\right]}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{V+i\Omega}{2}\right]}}(\rho_{\textbf{m},V}^{(1/2)})^{2}\,.

Here, in (i), we exploited (
134
). In (ii), we exploited that
\Tr[e^{-\frac{1}{2}\hat{\textbf{R}}^{\intercal}(H_{V}/2)\textbf{R}}]=\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{V^{(1/2)}+i\Omega}{2}\right]}
, which follows by using the definition of
\rho_{\textbf{m},V}^{(1/2)}
in (
136
) and using that
\Tr\rho_{\textbf{m},V}^{(1/2)}=1
. Finally, in (iii) we used the definition of
\rho_{\textbf{m},V}^{(1/2)}
in (
136
).

Consequently, we have that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Tr[\rho_{\textbf{m},V}\rho_{\textbf{t},W}^{-1}]

\displaystyle=\frac{\det\!\left[\frac{V^{(1/2)}+i\Omega}{2}\right]}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{V+i\Omega}{2}\right]}}\Tr[(\rho_{\textbf{m},V}^{(1/2)})^{2}\rho_{\textbf{t},W}^{-1}]

(141)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(iv)}}}}{{=}}\frac{\det\!\left[\frac{V^{(1/2)}+i\Omega}{2}\right]}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{V+i\Omega}{2}\right]}}\frac{\det\!\left[\frac{W+i\Omega}{2}\right]}{\det\!\left[\frac{V^{(1/2)}+i\Omega}{2}\right]}\frac{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{V+i\Omega}{2}\right]}}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{W-V}{2}\right]}}e^{(\textbf{m}-\textbf{t})^{\intercal}(W-V)^{-1}(\textbf{m}-\textbf{t})}

\displaystyle=\frac{\det\!\left[\frac{W+i\Omega}{2}\right]}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{W-V}{2}\right]}}e^{(\textbf{m}-\textbf{t})^{\intercal}(W-V)^{-1}(\textbf{m}-\textbf{t})}\,,

where in (iv) we exploited Lemma
38
.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
36
.

Proof of Theorem
36
.

Note that for all
t\geq 0
it holds that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptP_{>M}\coloneqq\Tr[(\mathbb{1}-\Pi_{M})\rho]\leq\Tr[e^{t\hat{N}_{n}}\rho]e^{-tM}\,,

(142)

where we exploited the operator inequality

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\mathbb{1}-\Pi_{M}\leq e^{t\hat{N}_{n}}e^{-tM}\,,

(143)

which can be readily verified in Fock basis, where
\hat{N}_{n}
denotes the photon number operator. We can minimise with respect to
t>0
the right-hand side of (
162
) in order to optimise the bound:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptP_{>M}\leq\inf_{t>0}\Tr[e^{t\hat{N}_{n}}\rho]e^{-tM}\,.

(144)

However, note that the term
\Tr[e^{t\hat{N}_{n}}\rho]
might diverge if
t
is sufficiently large (e.g. take
\rho
to be a thermal state with strictly positive temperature). Hence, if there exists a sufficiently small
\bar{t}>0
such that
\Tr[e^{\bar{t}\hat{N}_{n}}\rho]
is finite, then
P_{>M}
converges to zero
exponentially
fast in
M
. We now show that, if
\rho
is a Gaussian state, such a
\bar{t}
actually exists. To this end, let us note that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Tr[e^{t\hat{N}_{n}}\rho]

\displaystyle=(1-e^{-t})^{n}\Tr\left[\left(\tau^{\otimes n}_{\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}}\right)^{-1}\rho\right]\,,

(145)

where we introduced the thermal state
\tau_{\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}}
with mean photon number equal to
\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}
, which can be expressed as

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\left(\tau_{\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}}\right)^{\otimes n}=(1-e^{-t})^{n}\,e^{-t\hat{N}_{n}}\,.

(146)

Now, we want to exploit the formula for the overlap between a Gaussian state and the inverse of a Gaussian state reported in Lemma
39
. Since the covariance matrix of
\tau^{\otimes n}_{\frac{1}{e^{t}-1}}
is given by
\coth(t/2)\mathbb{1}
, the hypothesis of Lemma
39
is satisfied if
t
is such that
\coth(t/2)\mathbb{1}-V>0
, i.e.
\coth(t/2)>\|V\|_{\infty}
. Hence, thanks to Lemma
39
, for any
t<2\mathrm{arccoth}(\|V\|_{\infty})
it holds that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Tr[e^{t\hat{N}_{n}}\rho]

\displaystyle=(1-e^{-t})^{n}\frac{\det\!\left[\frac{\coth(t/2)\mathbb{1}+i\Omega}{2}\right]}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{\coth(t/2)\mathbb{1}-V}{2}\right]}}e^{\textbf{m}^{\intercal}(\coth(t/2)\mathbb{1}-V)^{-1}\textbf{m}}

(147)

\displaystyle=\left(\coth(t/2)-1\right)^{n}\frac{e^{\textbf{m}^{\intercal}(\coth(t/2)\mathbb{1}-V)^{-1}\textbf{m}}}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\coth(t/2)\mathbb{1}-V\right]}}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\textbf{m}^{\intercal}(\coth(t/2)\mathbb{1}-V)^{-1}\textbf{m}}}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{\coth(t/2)\mathbb{1}-V}{\coth(t/2)-1}\right]}}

Hence, (
144
) implies that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptP_{>M}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{0<t<2\mathrm{arccoth}(\|V\|_{\infty})}\frac{e^{\textbf{m}^{\intercal}(\coth(t/2)\mathbb{1}-V)^{-1}\textbf{m}}}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{\coth(t/2)\mathbb{1}-V}{\coth(t/2)-1}\right]}}\,e^{-tM}

(148)

\displaystyle=\inf_{x>\|V\|_{\infty}}\frac{e^{\textbf{m}^{\intercal}(x\mathbb{1}-V)^{-1}\textbf{m}}}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{x\mathbb{1}-V}{x-1}\right]}}\,e^{-2\,\mathrm{arccoth}(x)\,M}\,,

which proves (
128
).

Moreover, note that Lemma
37
guarantees that
1+2N+2\sqrt{N^{2}+N}\geq\|V\|_{\infty}
, where
N
is the mean photon number of the state. Hence, since
8N+4>1+2N+2\sqrt{N^{2}+N}
for all
N\geq 0
, we can choose the value of
x
in the above minimisation problem to be

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptx

\displaystyle\coloneqq 8N+4\,,

(149)

so that we can conclude that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptP_{>M}\leq\frac{e^{\textbf{m}^{\intercal}\left[(8N+4)\mathbb{1}-V\right]^{-1}\textbf{m}}}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{(8N+4)\mathbb{1}-V}{8N+3}\right]}}\,e^{-2\,\mathrm{arccoth}(8N+4)\,M}\,.

(150)

Moreover, by exploiting the elementary inequality
\mathrm{arccoth}(x)\geq\frac{1}{x}
, which is valid for any
x\geq 1
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptP_{>M}\leq\frac{e^{\textbf{m}^{\intercal}\left[(8N+4)\mathbb{1}-V\right]^{-1}\textbf{m}}}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{(8N+4)\mathbb{1}-V}{8N+3}\right]}}\,e^{-M/(4N+2)}\,.

(151)

Moreover, note that
N
can be expressed in terms of the first moment and covariance matrix as
N=\frac{\Tr[V-\mathbb{1}]}{4}+\frac{\|m\|_{2}^{2}}{2}
, as it can be proved by substituting the definitions of
V
,
m
, and
\hat{N}_{n}
. By using

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt(8N+4)\mathbb{1}\geq(4N+2)\mathbb{1}\geq\left(1+2N+2\sqrt{N^{2}+N}\right)\mathbb{1}\geq V\,,

(152)

as guaranteed by Lemma
37
, it holds that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\textbf{m}^{\intercal}\left[(8N+4)\mathbb{1}-V\right]^{-1}\textbf{m}

\displaystyle\leq\textbf{m}^{\intercal}\left[(8N+4)\mathbb{1}-V\right]^{-1}\textbf{m}

(153)

\displaystyle\leq\textbf{m}^{\intercal}\textbf{m}(4N+2)^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq\|m\|_{2}^{2}(2\|m\|_{2}^{2}+2)^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq 1/2\,,

and

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\det\!\left[\frac{(8N+4)\mathbb{1}-V}{8N+3}\right]\geq\det\!\left[\frac{4N+2}{8N+3}\mathbb{1}\right]\geq 2^{-2n}\,.

(154)

Consequently, we conclude that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptP_{>M}\leq\frac{e^{\textbf{m}^{\intercal}\left[(8N+4)\mathbb{1}-V\right]^{-1}\textbf{m}}}{\sqrt{\det\!\left[\frac{(8N+4)\mathbb{1}-V}{8N+3}\right]}}\,e^{-M/(4N+2)}\leq 2^{n}e^{-M/(4N+2)+1/2}\,.

(155)

∎

B.2
Trace-distance tail bound on Gaussian states

In this subsection, we solve the following problem. What is the trace-distance error in approximating a Gaussian state with its finite-dimensional approximation consisting of at most
M
photons? More precisely, given a Gaussian state specified by its first moment
m
and covariance matrix
V
, how to estimate the trace distance between itself and its projection onto the finite-dimensional subspace spanned by all the Fock states with at most
M
photons, as a function of
m
,
V
, and
M
?

Approximating a Gaussian state with a finite-dimensional state can be useful not only from an analytical perspective — as we will see in Section
C
— but also from a numerical one. Indeed, to numerically deal with an infinite-dimensional state, one usually truncates the Fock basis expansion of the state up to a certain photon cut-off. Due to the Holevo–Helstrom theorem
[
65
,
66
]
, the most meaningful way to measure the error incurred in such an approximation is given by the
trace-distance error
, defined as the trace distance between the original state and its truncated approximation. Hence, it is important to estimate such a trace-distance error in terms of the photon cut-off. Notably, the forthcoming Theorem
40
establishes that, for Gaussian states, such a trace-distance error decreases exponentially with the photon cut-off. This result has both analytical and numerical significance: analytically, it provides a rigorous and efficiently computable bound on the trace-distance error; numerically, it aids in estimating the trace-distance error when storing the truncated density matrix of a Gaussian state in a computer. Note that, in general, if one wants to numerically analyse properties of states prepared by applying general non-Gaussian operations to a Gaussian state, one necessarily need to make finite-dimensional approximations of the state.

Let us formalise the problem mathematically. Let us define the finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\pazocal{H}_{M}
spanned by all the
n
-mode Fock states with total number of photons less than
M
:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\pazocal{H}_{M}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{span}\!\left\{\ket{\textbf{k}}:\,\,\textbf{k}\in\mathds{N}^{n}\,\,,\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}\leq M\right\}

(156)

where
\ket{\textbf{k}}
is the
n
-mode Fock state defined by
\ket{\textbf{k}}=\ket{k_{1}}\otimes\ket{k_{2}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\ket{k_{n}}
. We dub the integer
M
as
photon cut-off
. Moreover, we dub the projection of
\rho
onto
\pazocal{H}_{M}
, defined as

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\rho_{M}\coloneqq\frac{\Pi_{M}\rho\Pi_{M}}{\Tr[\Pi_{M}\rho\Pi_{M}]}\,,

(157)

where
\Pi_{M}
is the projector onto
\pazocal{H}_{M}
, as
truncated approximation
of
\rho
. Given a Gaussian state
\rho
with first moment
m
and covariance matrix
V
, our goal is to find an upper bound on the trace distance
\frac{1}{2}\|\rho-\rho_{M}\|_{1}
between
\rho
and its truncated approximation
\rho_{M}
in terms of the first moment
m
,
V
, and
M
. In the following theorem, we establish such an upper bound.

Theorem 40

((Trace-distance tail bound on Gaussian states))
.

Let
\rho
be an
n
-mode Gaussian state with first moment
m
and covariance matrix
V
. Let
\rho_{M}\coloneqq\frac{\Pi_{M}\rho\Pi_{M}}{\Tr[\Pi_{M}\rho\Pi_{M}]}
be the projection of
\rho
onto the Hilbert space spanned by all the
n
-mode Fock states with photon number less than
M
, where
\Pi_{M}
is the projector on such a Hilbert space:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Pi_{M}\coloneqq\sum_{\textbf{k}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}:\,\sum_{i=1}^{n}k_{i}\leq M}\ket{\textbf{k}}\!\!\bra{\textbf{k}}\,,

(158)

with
\ket{\textbf{k}}=\ket{k_{1}}\otimes\ket{k_{2}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\ket{k_{n}}
being the
n
-mode Fock state. Then, the trace distance between
\rho
and its truncated approximation
\rho_{M}
converges exponentially fast to zero in the photon cut-off
M
, with exponential lifetime given by the mean photon number
N
. Specifically, such a trace distance can be upper bounded as

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\frac{1}{2}\left\|\rho-\rho_{M}\right\|_{1}\leq c\,e^{-M/(8N+4)}\,,

(159)

where
c
denotes a finite quantity defined in terms of the first moment
m
and covariance matrix
V
as

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptc

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{e^{\frac{1}{2}\textbf{m}^{\intercal}((8N+4)\mathbb{1}-V)^{-1}\textbf{m}}}{\left(\det\!\left[\frac{(8N+4)\mathbb{1}-V}{8N+3}\right]\right)^{1/4}}\,\leq 2^{n/2}e^{1/4},

(160)

and
N
denotes the mean photon number given by
N=\frac{\Tr[V-\mathbb{1}]}{4}+\frac{\|\textbf{m}\|_{2}^{2}}{2}
.

A strictly tighter bound can be obtained in terms of the following one-parameter optimisation problem:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\frac{1}{2}\left\|\rho-\rho_{M}\right\|_{1}\leq\inf_{x>\|V\|_{\infty}}\frac{e^{\frac{1}{2}\textbf{m}^{\intercal}(x\mathbb{1}-V)^{-1}\textbf{m}}}{\left(\det\!\left[\frac{x\mathbb{1}-V}{x-1}\right]\right)^{1/4}}e^{-M\mathrm{arccoth}(x)}\,,

(161)

where
\|V\|_{\infty}
denotes the operator norm of
V
.

Proof.

Gentle Measurement Lemma
[
11
, Lemma 6.15]
establishes that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\frac{1}{2}\left\|\rho-\frac{\Pi_{M}\rho\Pi_{M}}{\Tr[\Pi_{M}\rho\Pi_{M}]}\right\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\Tr[(\mathbb{1}-\Pi_{M})\rho]}\,,

(162)

i.e. it holds that
\frac{1}{2}\|\rho-\rho_{M}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{P_{>M}}
, where we introduced the probability
P_{>M}
that
\rho
has more than
M
photons. Hence, the statement directly follows by applying Theorem
36
.
∎

As an example of application of the trace-distance tail bound on Gaussian states presented in Theorem
40
, in the next subsection we design an efficient algorithm to estimate the trace distance between two Gaussian states.

B.3
Algorithm to estimate the trace distance between two Gaussian states

Calculating the trace distance between two Gaussian states is important, as the trace distance is the most meaningful notion of distance between quantum states
[
65
,
66
]
and Gaussian states are omnipresent in nature. However, calculating exactly such a trace distance is impossible in general, as the difference of two Gaussian states is a non-Gaussian operator, and there is no way to compute in general the trace norm of a non-Gaussian (infinite-dimensional) operator. Therefore, it is necessary to come up with methods to
approximate
, up to a fixed precision, the trace distance between two Gaussian states. While recent works have established both lower
[
53
]
and upper
[
51
,
52
,
53
,
54
,
67
]
bounds on the trace distance between two Gaussian states, there remains no known algorithm capable of computing such a trace distance up to a fixed precision. Here, we fill this gap, by providing the first algorithm to compute the trace distance between Gaussian states up to a fixed precision.

Our algorithm leverages the trace-distance tail bound on Gaussian states introduced in Theorem
40
. Specifically, by denoting as
\rho_{\textbf{m},V}
the Gaussian state with first moment
m
and covariance matrix
t
, the algorithm exploits the fact that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\frac{1}{2}\left\|\rho_{\textbf{m},V}-\frac{\Pi_{M}\rho_{\textbf{m},V}\Pi_{M}}{\Tr[\Pi_{M}\rho_{\textbf{m},V}\Pi_{M}]}\right\|_{1}\leq 2^{n/2}e^{1/4}e^{-M/(8N+4)}

(163)

where
N
denotes the mean photon number given by

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptN=\frac{\Tr[V-\mathbb{1}]}{4}+\frac{\|\textbf{m}\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\,.

(164)

The algorithm is given below and its correctness is proved in Theorem
41
.

Algorithm 2

(Computation of the trace distance
\frac{1}{2}\|\rho_{\textbf{m},V}-\rho_{\textbf{t},W}\|_{1}
between two Gaussian states
\rho_{\textbf{m},V}
and
\rho_{\textbf{t},W}
, given their first moments
\textbf{m},\textbf{t}
and covariance matrices
V,W
.

1:

First moments
\textbf{m},\textbf{t}
; covariance matrices
V,W
; error tolerance
\epsilon>0
.

2:

Approximation
\tilde{d}
such that
\left|\frac{1}{2}\|\rho_{\textbf{m},V}-\rho_{\textbf{t},W}\|_{1}-\tilde{d}\right|\leq\epsilon

3:

Use Eq. (
164
) to find the maximum
N
between the mean photon number of
\rho_{\textbf{m},V}
and the one of
\rho_{\textbf{t},W}
.

4:

Find
M
large enough so that the right-hand side of Eq. (
163
) is smaller than
\epsilon/3
. A value of
M=\pazocal{O}\!\left((n+1)(N+1)\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\right)\right)
is sufficient.

5:

Calculate the matrix elements of
\rho_{\textbf{m},V}
with respect to those Fock states which have number of photons not larger than
M
(e.g. via the methods introduced in
[
68
]
). With such matrix elements, construct a finite-dimensional matrix
A_{1}
and set
\rho_{1}\coloneqq A_{1}/\Tr A_{1}
.

6:

Analogously, calculate the matrix elements of
\rho_{\textbf{t},W}
with respect to the same basis as above, and construct the corresponding finite-dimensional matrix
A_{2}
. Finally, set
\rho_{2}\coloneqq A_{2}/\Tr A_{2}
.

7:

Compute the trace distance between the finite dimensional matrices
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
up to an error
\epsilon/3
.

8:

return
the estimate of the above trace distance.

Theorem 41

((Algorithm to approximate the trace distance between Gaussian states))
.

Let
\rho_{\textbf{m},V}
and
\rho_{\textbf{t},W}
be
n
-mode Gaussian states with first moments
\textbf{m},\textbf{t}
and covariance matrices
V,W
. Given in input the first moments
\textbf{m},\textbf{t}
, covariance matrices
V,W
, and error tolerance
\epsilon>0
, the above algorithm outputs
\tilde{d}
such that it approximates the trace distance between the two states up to an error
\epsilon
:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\left|\frac{1}{2}\|\rho_{\textbf{m},V}-\rho_{\textbf{t},W}\|_{1}-\tilde{d}\right|\leq\epsilon\,.

(165)

Moreover, the runtime of the algorithm scales exponentially in the number of modes
n
; specifically as

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\pazocal{O}\!\left(2^{6}(n+1)E\log(2/\epsilon)\right)^{3n}+t_{\mathrm{Fock}}\,,

(166)

where
E
is the maximum mean energy per mode of the two states, i.e.

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptE\coloneqq\frac{1}{n}\max\left(\frac{\Tr V}{4}+\frac{\|\textbf{m}\|_{2}^{2}}{2},\frac{\Tr W}{4}+\frac{\|\textbf{t}\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\right)\,,

(167)

while
t_{\mathrm{Fock}}
is the time required to calculate the Fock basis matrix elements in Step 3 and Step 4. Additionally, the notation
\pazocal{O}
hides logarithmic factors in
E
.

Proof.

In this proof we will use the notation introduced in the algorithm in Table
2
, Eq. (
165
) trivially follows by multiple applications of triangle inequality and by observing that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\|\rho_{2}-\rho_{1}\|_{1}=\left\|\frac{\Pi_{M}\rho_{\textbf{t},W}\Pi_{M}}{\Tr[\Pi_{M}\rho_{\textbf{t},W}\Pi_{M}]}-\frac{\Pi_{M}\rho_{\textbf{m},V}\Pi_{M}}{\Tr[\Pi_{M}\rho_{\textbf{m},V}\Pi_{M}]}\right\|_{1}\,.

(168)

Let us estimate the runtime of the algorithm. Let
N_{1}=\frac{\Tr[V-\mathbb{1}]}{4}+\frac{\|m\|_{2}^{2}}{2}
,
N-2=\frac{\Tr[W-\mathbb{1}]}{4}+\frac{\|t\|_{2}^{2}}{2}
. At Step 2,
M
is chosen such that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt2^{n}e^{-M/(8N_{1}+4)+1/4}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon/3\,,

(169)

\displaystyle 2^{n}e^{-M/(8N_{2}+4)+1/4}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon/3\,,

i.e. such that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptM\geq\max(8N_{1}+4,8N_{2}+4)\log\frac{3e^{1/4}\cdot 2^{n}}{\epsilon}\,.

(170)

Hence, since
nE=N+n/2
, it suffices to take

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptM\coloneqq\lceil 8nE\log\frac{3e^{1/4}\cdot 2^{n}}{\epsilon}\rceil\,.

(171)

Moreover, Step 5 requires the diagonalisation of the finite dimensional matrix
\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}
, a task well-known to be achieved in a time scaling as
\pazocal{O}(d^{3})
, where
d
denotes the dimension of the matrix
\rho_{1}-\rho_{2}
. By definition, such a dimension
d
is given by the total number of
n
-mode Fock states with number of photons not larger than
M
. An elementary counting argument shows that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptd=\binom{n+M}{n}\,.

(172)

In particular, by exploiting the elementary inequality
\binom{a}{b}\leq(e\frac{a}{b})^{b}
valid for any
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptd\leq\left(e+e\frac{\lceil 4nE\log\frac{3\cdot 2^{n}}{e\epsilon}\rceil}{n}\right)^{n}\leq\left(e+\frac{e}{n}+4eE\log\frac{3e^{1/4}\cdot 2^{n}}{\epsilon}\right)^{n}=\pazocal{O}\left(\!\left(2^{6}(n+1)E\log(2/\epsilon)\right)^{n}\right)\,.

(173)

Consequently, the runtime of Step 5 is
\pazocal{O}\left(\!\left(2^{6}(n+1)E\log(2/\epsilon)\right)^{3n}\right)
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Notably, the runtime
\pazocal{O}\!\left(\!\left(2^{6}(n+1)E\log(2/\epsilon)\right)^{3n}\right)
of the above algorithm, with its logarithmic dependence on the trace-distance error
\epsilon
, is a distinctive feature of Gaussian states. In contrast, as proved in Lemma
42
, a similar algorithm for computing the trace distance between two
n
-mode non-Gaussian states with precision error
\epsilon
has a significantly worse upper bound on the runtime of
\pazocal{O}\!\left(\!\left(\frac{16E}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)^{3n}\right)
.

Lemma 42
.

The trace distance between two
n
-mode (non-Gaussian) states can be computed up to a trace-distance error
\epsilon
in time
\pazocal{O}\!\left(\!\left(\frac{16E}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)^{3n}\right)
, where
E
denotes the maximum between the mean energy per mode of the two states.

Proof.

By denoting as
\Pi_{M}
the projector onto the Hilbert space spanned by all the
n
-mode Fock states with at most
M
photons, it holds that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\frac{1}{2}\left\|\rho-\frac{\Pi_{M}\rho\Pi_{M}}{\Tr[\Pi_{M}\rho\Pi_{M}]}\right\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{\Tr[(\mathbb{1}-\Pi_{M})\rho]}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\Tr[\hat{N}_{n}\rho]}{M}}\,,

(174)

where we used Gentle Measurement Lemma
[
11
, Lemma 6.15]
and the operator inequality
\mathbb{1}-\Pi_{M}\leq\frac{\hat{N}_{n}}{M}
.
Consequently, we have that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\frac{1}{2}\left\|\rho-\frac{\Pi_{M}\rho\Pi_{M}}{\Tr[\Pi_{M}\rho\Pi_{M}]}\right\|_{1}\leq\epsilon/3\,,

(175)

if the photon cut-off
M
satisfies

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptM\geq\frac{9\Tr[\hat{N}_{n}\rho]}{\epsilon^{2}}\,.

(176)

Let us now follow the same reasoning as the proof of Theorem
41
. Thanks to Eq. (
176
), in order to have both the two states approximated with trace-distance error
\varepsilon/3
via their projection onto the Hilbert space spanned by all the Fock states with at most
M
photons, it suffices to choose

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptM\coloneqq\left\lceil\frac{9nE}{\epsilon^{2}}\right\rceil\,,

(177)

where
E
is the maximum mean energy per mode of the two states. The dimension
d
of such Hilbert space is

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptd=\binom{M+n}{n}\leq\left(e\frac{M+n}{n}\right)^{n}\leq\left(e+\frac{e}{n}+\frac{9eE}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)^{n}=\pazocal{O}\!\left(\!\left(\frac{16E}{\epsilon^{2}}\right)^{n}\right)\,.

(178)

Hence, it suffices to diagonalise a matrix of dimension
d
, which has a runtime of
O(d^{3})
.
∎

Appendix C
Non-asymptotic analysis of quantum communication across Gaussian channels

This section aims to establish an easily computable lower bound on the
n
-shot quantum capacity,
n
-shot two-way quantum capacity, and
n
-shot secret-key capacity of an arbitrary Gaussian channel.

As we will see below, to obtain an easily computable lower bound on the
n
-shot capacities, it suffices to lower bound the smooth max relative entropy
D_{\max}^{\varepsilon}
defined in Definition
24
in terms of quantities that do not require optimisations. This can be achieved by applying the
asymptotic equipartition property
proved in
[
61
, Theorem 9]
, which holds in finite dimensions. This property was recently generalised to infinite dimensions in
[
63
, Theorem 6.2]
, while an earlier, non-tight infinite-dimensional generalisation was presented in
[
62
, Proposition 8]
.

We will now state the infinite-dimensional generalisation of the asymptotic equipartition property from
[
63
, Theorem 6.2]
, which is formulated within the framework of
von Neumann algebras
. Although we do not need to introduce this framework in our paper, it is relevant to mention that in the context of von Neumann algebras, any operator
\sigma
with a bounded trace is also
normal

[
63
]
. Therefore, we can apply
[
63
, Theorem 6.2]
under the assumption that the operator
\sigma
has a bounded trace.

Lemma 43

((Infinite-dimensional asymptotic equipartition property
[
63
]
))
.

Let
\rho
be a state and let
\sigma
be an arbitrary positive semi-definite operator with bounded trace. For any
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
with
n\geq 2\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
, we have

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptD_{\max}^{\varepsilon}(\rho^{\otimes n}\|\sigma^{\otimes n})\leq nD(\rho\|\sigma)+4\sqrt{n}\log_{2}({\bar{\eta}})\sqrt{\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)}\,,

(179)

where

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\bar{\eta}\coloneqq\sqrt{2^{D_{3/2}(\rho\|\sigma)}}+\sqrt{2^{-D_{1/2}(\rho\|\sigma)}}+1\,,

(180)

where
D_{1/2}
and
D_{3/2}
denote the Petz Rényi relative entropy defined in Definition
21
.

C.1
Quantum capacity

In this paragraph, we prove a lower bound on the
n
-shot quantum capacity of Gaussian channels, presented in the forthcoming Theorem
45
.

We start by stating the following result regarding finite-dimensional channels, which establishes a lower bound on the one-shot quantum capacity in terms of the smooth conditional max-entropy.

Lemma 44

((Lower bound on the one-shot quantum capacity of finite-dimensional channels
[
11
]
))
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a finite-dimensional quantum channel. For all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\delta\in(0,1)
, and
\eta\in[0,\frac{\sqrt{\delta}}{4})
, the one-shot quantum capacity of
\mathcal{N}
can be lower bounded as:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptQ^{(\varepsilon)}(\mathcal{N})\geq-H_{\max}^{\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{\delta}}{4}-\eta}(A|B)_{\mathcal{N}_{A^{\prime}\to B}(\psi_{AA^{\prime}})}+\log_{2}\!\left(\eta^{4}(1-\delta)\right)

(181)

for any pure state
\psi_{AA^{\prime}}
, where
H_{\max}^{\varepsilon}
denotes the smooth conditional max-entropy defined in Definition
26
.

We are now ready to present our lower bound.

Theorem 45

((Lower bound on the
n
-shot quantum capacity of Gaussian channels))
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a Gaussian channel. Let
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
,
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\eta\in[0,\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{\delta}}{4})
, and
n\in\mathds{N}
such that
n\geq 2\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{2}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)
. For any pure Gaussian state
\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}
, the
n
-shot quantum capacity of
\mathcal{N}
satisfies the following lower bound:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptQ^{(\varepsilon,n)}(\mathcal{N})\geq nI_{c}(A\,\rangle\,B)_{\Psi}-\sqrt{n}4\log_{2}({\bar{\eta}})\sqrt{\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{2}{\left(\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{\delta}}{4}-\eta\right)^{2}}\right)}+\log_{2}\!\left(\eta^{4}(1-\delta)\right)\,,

(182)

where

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\bar{\eta}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sqrt{2^{H_{1/2}(A|B)_{\Psi}}}+\sqrt{2^{H_{1/2}(A|E)_{\Psi}}}+1\,,

(183)

\displaystyle\Psi_{ABE}

\displaystyle\coloneqq V_{A^{\prime}\to BE}\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}(V_{A^{\prime}\to BE})^{\dagger}\,,

and
V_{A^{\prime}\to BE}
is a Stinespring dilation isometry associated with
\mathcal{N}_{A^{\prime}\to B}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptI_{c}(A\,\rangle\,B)_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\coloneqq S(B)_{\Psi}-S(AB)_{\Psi}\,,

(184)

\displaystyle H_{1/2}(A|B)_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2\log_{2}\!\left(\Tr\sqrt{\Psi_{AB}}\,\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{B}}\right)\,,

\displaystyle H_{1/2}(A|E)_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\coloneqq 2\log_{2}\!\left(\Tr\sqrt{\Psi_{AE}}\,\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{E}}\right)\,

are finite quantities denoting coherent information and conditional Petz Rényi entropies. In particular, by choosing
\delta=\frac{1}{4}
and
\eta=\frac{\varepsilon}{16}
, we get

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptQ^{(\varepsilon,n)}(\mathcal{N})\geq nI_{c}(A\,\rangle\,B)_{\Psi}-\sqrt{n}4\log_{2}({\bar{\eta}})\sqrt{\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{2^{9}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)}-\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{2^{18}}{3\varepsilon^{4}}\right)\,.

(185)

In particular, if the input state
\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}
satisfies the energy constraint
\Tr[\hat{N}_{A^{\prime}}\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}]\leq N_{s}
, where
\hat{N}_{A^{\prime}}
is the photon number operator on
A^{\prime}
, then the right-hand side of (
185
) constitutes also a lower bound on the energy-constrained
n
-shot quantum capacity
Q^{(\varepsilon,n)}(\mathcal{N},N_{s})
.

Proof.

Currently available lower bounds on the one-shot quantum capacity are formulated in the finite-dimensional setting, e.g. the one provided above in Lemma
44
. Therefore, we will begin by constructing a sequence of finite-dimensional approximations of the infinite-dimensional channel
\mathcal{N}
and then apply the finite-dimensional lower bound reported in Lemma
44
.

Let us introduce some relevant notations. Given a continuous-variable system
S
, let us denote as
m_{S}
the number of modes of
S
. Moreover, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let us define the finite-dimensional Hilbert space
\pazocal{H}^{S}_{k}
spanned by all the
m_{S}
-mode Fock states on
S
with total number of photons less than
k
:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\pazocal{H}^{S}_{k}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{span}\!\left\{\ket{\textbf{i}}_{S}:\,\,\textbf{i}\in\mathds{N}^{m_{S}}\,\,,\sum_{j=1}^{m_{S}}i_{j}\leq k\right\}\,,

(186)

where
\ket{\textbf{i}}
is the
{m_{S}}
-mode Fock state defined by

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\ket{\textbf{i}}_{S}=\ket{i_{1}}\otimes\ket{i_{2}}\otimes\ldots\otimes\ket{i_{m_{S}}}\,.

(187)

Let us define as
P^{S}_{k}
the projector on
\pazocal{H}^{S}_{k}
, and let us note that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Tr P^{S}_{k}=\dim\mathcal{H}_{k}^{S}=\left|\left\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{m_{S}}\in\mathds{N}\,,\quad\sum_{j=1}^{m_{S}}i_{j}\leq k\right\}\right|=\binom{k+m_{S}}{m_{S}}\,,

(188)

where in the last equality we used a standard combinatorial argument. In addition, let us consider a qudit system
S_{k}\cong\mathbb{C}^{\binom{k+m_{S}}{m_{S}}}
of dimension
\binom{k+m_{S}}{m_{S}}
and a co-isometry
V_{S\to S_{k}}
that maps the subspace
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{S}
into
S_{k}
. Moreover, let
\mathcal{V}_{S\to S_{k}}(\cdot)\coloneqq V_{S\to S_{k}}(\cdot)V_{S\to S_{k}}^{\dagger}
be the corresponding superoperator. Moreover, let us consider the projection of the state
\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}
induced by the projector
P_{k}^{A}
:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Phi^{(k)}_{A_{k}A^{\prime}}\coloneqq\mathcal{V}_{A\to A_{k}}\left(\frac{P_{k}^{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{A^{\prime}}\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}P_{k}^{A}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{A^{\prime}}}{\Tr[P_{k}^{A}\Phi_{A}]}\right)

(189)

Since
\Phi^{(k)}_{A_{k}A^{\prime}}
is a pure state and the system
A_{k}
has dimension
\binom{m_{A}+k}{m_{A}}
, the support of
\Tr_{A}\Phi^{(k)}_{A_{k}A^{\prime}}
is contained in a
\binom{m_{A}+k}{m_{A}}
-dimensional subspace of
A^{\prime}
. Let
V^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to A^{\prime}}
be the isometric embedding that maps
A^{\prime}_{k}\cong\mathbb{C}^{\binom{m_{A}+k}{m_{A}}}
into such a subspace, let
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to A^{\prime}}(\cdot)\coloneqq V^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to A^{\prime}}(\cdot)(V^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to A^{\prime}})^{\dagger}
be its associated channel, and let us introduce the following pure state on
A_{k}A^{\prime}_{k}
:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Phi^{(k)}_{A_{k}A^{\prime}_{k}}\coloneqq\mathcal{V}^{\prime\dagger}_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to A^{\prime}}(\Phi^{(k)}_{A_{k}A^{\prime}})\,,

(190)

where we denoted
\mathcal{V}^{\prime\dagger}_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to A^{\prime}}(\cdot)\coloneqq V^{\prime\dagger}_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to A^{\prime}}(\cdot)(V^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to A^{\prime}})
. Moreover, let us introduce the following
finite
-dimensional channel
\mathcal{N}^{(k)}:A^{\prime}_{k}\to B_{k}
, which constitutes a finite-dimensional approximation of the Gaussian channel
\mathcal{N}
:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\mathcal{N}^{(k)}_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to B_{k}}\coloneqq\mathcal{V}_{B\to B_{k}}\circ\mathcal{P}^{(k)}_{B\to B}\circ\mathcal{N}_{A^{\prime}\to B}\circ\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to A^{\prime}}\,,

(191)

where

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\mathcal{P}^{(k)}_{B\to B}(\cdot)\coloneqq P_{k}^{B}(\cdot)P^{B}_{k}+\Tr\!\left[(\mathbb{1}_{B}-P_{k}^{B})(\cdot)\right]\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{B}\,.

(192)

We can thus apply Lemma
44
to such a finite-dimensional channel to obtain

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptQ^{(\varepsilon,n)}(\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(i)}}}}{{\geq}}-H_{\max}^{\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{\delta}}{4}-\eta}(A^{\prime n}_{k}|B_{k}^{n})_{\omega_{A_{k}B_{k}}^{\otimes n}}+\log_{2}\!\left(\eta^{4}(1-\delta)\right)\,,

(193)

where
\omega_{A_{k}B_{k}}\coloneqq\mathbb{1}_{A_{k}}\otimes\mathcal{N}^{(k)}_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to B_{k}}(\Phi^{(k)}_{A_{k}A_{k}^{\prime}})
. Now, we want to lower bound
Q^{(\varepsilon)}
using the asymptotic equipartition property reported in Lemma
43
. To do so, we need to write the above lower bound on
Q^{(\varepsilon)}
in terms of the smooth conditional max-entropy
D_{\max}^{\varepsilon}
. To this end, let us write the Stinespring dilation of the above channels. Note that the isometry

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptW^{(k)}_{B\to BE^{\prime}}\coloneqq P_{k}^{B}\otimes\ket{0}_{E^{\prime}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{m_{B}}\\
\sum_{j=1}^{m_{B}}i_{j}\not\leq k\end{subarray}}\ket{0}\!\!\bra{\textbf{i}}_{B}\otimes\ket{\textbf{i}}_{E^{\prime}}

(194)

constitutes a Stinespring dilation isometry for
\mathcal{P}^{(k)}_{B\to B}
. Consequently, we can construct a Stinespring dilation isometry for
\mathcal{N}^{(k)}_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to B_{k}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptV_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to B^{\prime}_{k}EE^{\prime}}\coloneqq V_{B\to B_{k}}W^{(k)}_{B\to BE^{\prime}}V_{A^{\prime}\to BE}V^{\prime}_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to A^{\prime}}\,.

(195)

By setting

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Psi^{(k)}_{A_{k}B_{k}EE^{\prime}}\coloneqq V_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to B^{\prime}_{k}EE^{\prime}}\Phi^{(k)}_{A_{k}A^{\prime}_{k}}V_{A^{\prime}_{k}\to B^{\prime}_{k}EE^{\prime}}^{\dagger}\,,

(196)

we have that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptQ^{(\varepsilon,n)}(\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle\geq-H_{\max}^{\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{\delta}}{4}-\eta}(A^{\prime n}_{k}|B_{k}^{n})_{\omega_{A_{k}B_{k}}^{\otimes n}}+\log_{2}\!\left(\eta^{4}(1-\delta)\right)

(197)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(i)}}}}{{=}}-H_{\max}^{\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{\delta}}{4}-\eta}(A^{\prime n}_{k}|B_{k}^{n})_{(\Psi^{(k)}_{A_{k}B_{k}EE^{\prime}})^{\otimes n}}+\log_{2}\!\left(\eta^{4}(1-\delta)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(ii)}}}}{{=}}H_{\min}^{\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{\delta}}{4}-\eta}(A^{\prime n}_{k}|E^{n}E^{\prime n})_{(\Psi^{(k)}_{A_{k}B_{k}EE^{\prime}})^{\otimes n}}+\log_{2}\!\left(\eta^{4}(1-\delta)\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(iii)}}}}{{\geq}}-D_{\max}^{\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{\delta}}{4}-\eta}\left((\Psi^{(k)}_{A_{k}EE^{\prime}})^{\otimes n}\|\mathbb{1}_{A_{k}^{n}}\otimes(\Psi^{(k)}_{EE^{\prime}})^{\otimes n}\right)+\log_{2}\!\left(\eta^{4}(1-\delta)\right)\,.

Here, in (i) we just used the fact that

\omega_{A_{k}B_{k}}=\Tr_{EE^{\prime}}\Psi^{(k)}_{A_{k}B_{k}EE^{\prime}}
together with our notation of omitting partial traces in the subscript of entropies; in (ii) we used the duality relation between the smooth conditional max-entropy and the smooth conditional min-entropy reported in Lemma
27
; and in (iii) we leveraged the definition of smooth conditional min-entropy in Definition
25
.

We are now ready to apply the asymptotic equipartition property reported in Lemma
43
. Note that the hypothesis of Lemma
43
is satisfied because the operator
\sigma\coloneqq\mathbb{1}_{A_{k}}\otimes\Psi^{(k)}_{EE^{\prime}}
has bounded trace (specifically, its trace equals
\binom{m_{A}+k}{m_{A}}
). Consequently, we obtain that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptQ^{(\varepsilon,n)}(\mathcal{N})

\displaystyle\geq-nD\left(\Psi^{(k)}_{A_{k}EE^{\prime}}\|\mathbb{1}_{A_{k}}\otimes\Psi^{(k)}_{EE^{\prime}}\right)

(198)

\displaystyle-{\sqrt{n}}4\log_{2}\!\left(\sqrt{2^{D_{3/2}(\Psi_{A_{k}EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\|\mathbb{1}_{A_{k}}\otimes\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)})}}+\sqrt{2^{-D_{1/2}(\Psi_{A_{k}EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\|\mathbb{1}_{A_{k}}\otimes\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)})}}+1\right)\sqrt{\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{2}{\left(\frac{\varepsilon\sqrt{\delta}}{4}-\eta\right)^{2}}\right)}+\log_{2}\!\left(\eta^{4}(1-\delta)\right)\,.

The above inequality holds for any
k
. Now, we would like to take the limit
k\rightarrow\infty
in the above inequality in order to make the following substitution

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Psi_{A_{k}B_{k}EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\leftrightarrow\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}\coloneqq\Psi_{ABE}^{\dagger}\otimes\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{E^{\prime}}\,,

(199)

where
\Psi_{ABE}\coloneqq V_{A^{\prime}\to BE}\Phi_{AA^{\prime}}V_{A^{\prime}\to BE}
. This limit procedure is far from trivial and requires careful analysis of the entropic quantities involved in (
198
). Let us start by showing that the limit
\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\xrightarrow{k\rightarrow\infty}\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}
holds in trace norm. To this end, let us observe that the state
\Psi_{A_{k}B_{k}EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}
can be written as

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\ket{\Psi_{A_{k}B_{k}EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}=V_{A\to A_{k}}\otimes V_{B\to B_{k}}\otimes\mathbb{1}_{EE^{\prime}}\ket{\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\,,

(200)

where we introduced

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\ket{\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\coloneqq\frac{P_{k}^{A}\otimes W_{B\to BE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\ket{\Psi_{ABE}}}{\sqrt{\Tr[P_{k}^{A}\Psi_{A}]}}\,,

(201)

and we used the vectorial notation
\psi=\ket{\psi}\!\!\bra{\psi}
for any pure state
\psi
. Since it will be useful for the following, we are now going to prove not only that the convergence
\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\xrightarrow{k\rightarrow\infty}\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}
holds in trace norm but also that such a convergence is exponentially fast in
k
:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\|\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}-\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}\|_{1}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{1-\Tr[\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}]}

(202)

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{1-\frac{\Tr[\Psi_{AB}P_{k}^{A}\otimes P_{k}^{B}]}{\Tr[\Psi_{A}P_{k}^{A}]}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{1-\Tr[\Psi_{AB}P_{k}^{A}\otimes P_{k}^{B}]}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{\Tr[\Psi_{AB}(\mathbb{1}_{A}-P_{k}^{A})\otimes P_{k}^{B}]+\Tr[\Psi_{AB}P_{k}^{A}\otimes(\mathbb{1}_{B}-P_{k}^{B})]+\Tr[\Psi_{AB}(\mathbb{1}_{A}-P_{k}^{A})\otimes(\mathbb{1}_{B}-P_{k}^{B})]}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(i)}}}}{{\leq}}2\sqrt{\Tr[\Psi_{A}(\mathbb{1}_{A}-P_{k}^{A})]+\Tr[\Psi_{B}(\mathbb{1}_{B}-P_{k}^{B})]+\sqrt{\Tr[\Psi_{A}(\mathbb{1}_{A}-P_{k}^{A})]\Tr[\Psi_{A}(\mathbb{1}_{A}-P_{k}^{A})]}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\sqrt{\Tr[\Psi_{A}(\mathbb{1}_{A}-P_{k}^{A})]}+\sqrt{\Tr[\Psi_{B}(\mathbb{1}_{B}-P_{k}^{B})])}\right)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(ii)}}}}{{=}}2\left(c_{\Psi_{A}}e^{-b_{\Psi_{A}}k}+c_{\Psi_{B}}e^{-b_{\Psi_{B}}k}\right)\,.

Here, in (i), we exploited that
P_{k}\leq\mathbb{1}
and Cauchy-Schwarz inequality; in (ii), we employed Theorem
40
, which implies that for any Gaussian state
\rho
there exist constants
c_{\rho}
and
b_{\rho}
such that
\sqrt{\Tr[\rho(\mathbb{1}-P_{k})]}\leq c_{\rho}e^{-b_{\rho}k}
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Consequently, we conclude that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\|\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}-\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}\|_{1}=0\,,

(203)

and that such a convergence is exponentially fast in
k
.

Note that can rewrite the entropic quantities involved in (
198
) in terms of
\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}
as follows:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptD\left(\Psi^{(k)}_{A_{k}EE^{\prime}}\|\mathbb{1}_{A_{k}}\otimes\Psi^{(k)}_{EE^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=D\left(\Psi^{(k)}_{AEE^{\prime}}\|\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Psi^{(k)}_{EE^{\prime}}\right)\,,

(204)

\displaystyle D_{1/2}(\Psi_{A_{k}EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\|\mathbb{1}_{A_{k}}\otimes\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)})

\displaystyle=D_{1/2}(\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\|\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)})\,,

\displaystyle D_{3/2}(\Psi_{A_{k}EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\|\mathbb{1}_{A_{k}}\otimes\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)})

\displaystyle=D_{3/2}(\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\|\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)})\,.

Hence, in order to prove the theorem, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}D\left(\Psi^{(k)}_{AEE^{\prime}}\|\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Psi^{(k)}_{EE^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=-I_{c}(A\,\rangle\,B)_{\Psi}\,,

(205)

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}D_{1/2}(\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\|\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)})

\displaystyle=-H_{1/2}(A|E)_{\Psi}\,,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}D_{3/2}(\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\|\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)})

\displaystyle=H_{1/2}(A|B)_{\Psi}\,.

Let us start with the proof of the first limit in (
205
). The term
D\left(\Psi^{(k)}_{AEE^{\prime}}\|\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Psi^{(k)}_{EE^{\prime}}\right)
can be expressed in terms of the conditional entropy as:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptD\left(\Psi^{(k)}_{AEE^{\prime}}\|\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Psi^{(k)}_{EE^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle=-S(\Psi^{(k)}_{AEE^{\prime}})+S(\Psi^{(k)}_{EE^{\prime}})

(206)

\displaystyle=-S(\Psi^{(k)}_{B})+S(\Psi^{(k)}_{AB})

\displaystyle=S(A|B)_{\Psi^{(k)}_{AB}}\,.

Moreover, by exploiting the monotonicity of the trace norm under partial traces
[
37
]
together with the fact that the limit
\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\xrightarrow{k\rightarrow\infty}\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}
holds in trace norm (as proved above in (
203
)), we have that the limit

\Psi_{AB}^{(k)}\xrightarrow{k\rightarrow\infty}\Psi_{AB}
holds in trace norm. In addition, since
\Psi_{AB}
is a Gaussian state, it follows that both
\Psi_{AB}
and
\Psi_{AB}^{(k)}
have bounded mean photon number. Consequently, since the conditional entropy is continuous with respect the trace norm over the set of states having bounded mean photon number
[
78
]
, it follows that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}S(A|B)_{\Psi^{(k)}_{AB}}=S(A|B)_{\Psi_{AB}}\,,

(207)

and we can thus conclude that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}D\left(\Psi^{(k)}_{AEE^{\prime}}\|\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Psi^{(k)}_{EE^{\prime}}\right)=S(A|B)_{\Psi}=-I_{c}(A\,\rangle\,B)_{\Psi}\,.

(208)

Let us now prove the second limit in (
205
):

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}D_{1/2}(\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\|\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)})

\displaystyle=-H_{1/2}(A|E)_{\Psi}\,.

(209)

Thanks to the definition of conditional Petz-Rényi entropy and to the fact that
\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}=\Psi_{AE}\otimes\ket{0}\!\!\bra{0}_{E^{\prime}}
, it suffices to prove that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\,\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\right]=\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\,\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\right]\,.

(210)

Note that
\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\,\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\right]=\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}P_{k}^{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\right]
and

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\,P_{k}^{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\right]

\displaystyle=\Tr\left[\left(\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}-\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\right)\,P_{k}^{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\right]

(211)

\displaystyle+\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\,\left(P_{k}^{A}-\mathbb{1}_{A}\right)\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\right]

\displaystyle+\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\,P_{k}^{A}\otimes\left(\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}-\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle+\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\,\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\right]\,.

Hence, in order to show (
210
), it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left|\Tr\left[\left(\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}-\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\right)\,P_{k}^{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\right]\right|

\displaystyle=0\,,

(212)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left|\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\,\left(P_{k}^{A}-\mathbb{1}_{A}\right)\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\right]\right|

\displaystyle=0\,,

(213)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left|\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\,P_{k}^{A}\otimes\left(\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}-\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle=0\,.

(214)

Let us start by showing the validity of (
212
):

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\left|\Tr\left[\left(\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}-\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\right)\,P_{k}^{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{E}^{(k)}}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(iii)}}}}{{\leq}}\left\|\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}-\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\right\|_{2}\left\|P_{k}^{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\right\|_{2}

(215)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(iv)}}}}{{=}}\left\|\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}-\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\right\|_{2}\sqrt{\binom{k+m_{A}}{m_{A}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(v)}}}}{{\leq}}\sqrt{\binom{k+m_{A}}{m_{A}}\|\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}-\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}\|_{1}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(vi)}}}}{{\leq}}\sqrt{\binom{k+m_{A}}{m_{A}}\|\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}-\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}\|_{1}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(vii)}}}}{{\leq}}\sqrt{\binom{k+m_{A}}{m_{A}}2\left(c_{\Psi_{A}}e^{-b_{\Psi_{A}}k}+c_{\Psi_{B}}e^{-b_{\Psi_{B}}k}\right)}\xrightarrow{k\rightarrow\infty}0\,.

Here, in (iii) we used Hölder’s inequality. In (iv), we exploited that
\left\|P_{k}^{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\right\|_{2}=\sqrt{\Tr[P_{k}^{A}]\Tr[\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}]}=\sqrt{\binom{k+m_{A}}{m_{A}}}
, where in the last equality we employed (
188
). In (v), we used that for any positive semi-definite operators
A,B\geq 0
it holds that
[
79
, Section X.6]

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\|A^{1/t}-B^{1/t}\|_{tp}\leq\|A-B\|^{1/t}_{p}\quad\text{for all $p,t\geq 1$\,.}

(216)

In (vi), we used the monotonicity of the trace norm under partial traces. Finally, in (vii), we exploited (
202
).

Now, let us show (
213
). We have that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\left|\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\,\left(P_{k}^{A}-\mathbb{1}_{A}\right)\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\right]\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\Tr\left[\left(P_{k}^{A}-\mathbb{1}_{A}\right)\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\right]\right|

(217)

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{m_{B}}\\
\sum_{j=1}^{m_{B}}i_{j}\not\leq k\end{subarray}}\bra{\textbf{i}}_{A}\Tr_{EE^{\prime}}\left[\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\,\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\right]\ket{\textbf{i}}_{A}\right|\xrightarrow{k\rightarrow\infty}0\,,

where in the last line we exploited that the series

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\textbf{i}\in\mathbb{N}^{m_{B}}\\
\sum_{j=1}^{m_{B}}i_{j}\leq k\end{subarray}}\bra{\textbf{i}}_{A}\Tr_{EE^{\prime}}\left[\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\,\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\right]\ket{\textbf{i}}_{A}

(218)

converges to
\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\,\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\right]
for
k\rightarrow\infty
, which is a finite quantity because
\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}
is a Gaussian state. Indeed, for any Gaussian state
\rho_{AB}
it holds that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\Tr[\sqrt{\rho_{AB}}\,\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\sqrt{\rho_{B}}]\leq\Tr[\sqrt{\rho_{AB}}]<\infty\,,

(219)

where in the first inequality we exploited that
\sqrt{\sigma}\leq\mathbb{1}
for any state
\sigma
, and in the second inequality we employed Lemma
32
, which states that
\Tr\sqrt{\sigma}<\infty
for any Gaussian state
\sigma
.

Now, let us show (
214
):

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\left|\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\,P_{k}^{A}\otimes\left(\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}-\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\right)\right]\right|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(i)}}}}{{\leq}}\left\|\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\right\|_{2}\left\|P_{k}^{A}\otimes\left(\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}-\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\right)\right\|_{2}

(220)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(ii)}}}}{{=}}\sqrt{\binom{k+m_{A}}{m_{A}}}\left\|\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}-\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\right\|_{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(iii)}}}}{{\leq}}\sqrt{\binom{k+m_{A}}{m_{A}}\|\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}-\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}\|_{1}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(iv)}}}}{{\leq}}\sqrt{\binom{k+m_{A}}{m_{A}}\|\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}-\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}\|_{1}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\scriptsize\mbox{(v)}}}}{{\leq}}\sqrt{\binom{k+m_{A}}{m_{A}}2\left(c_{\Psi_{A}}e^{-b_{\Psi_{A}}k}+c_{\Psi_{B}}e^{-b_{\Psi_{B}}k}\right)}\xrightarrow{k\rightarrow\infty}0\,.

Here, in (i), we used Hölder’s inequality. In (ii), we exploited the multiplicativity of the 2-norm under tensor product, Eq. (
188
), and the fact that
\|\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\|_{2}=1
.
In (iii), we used the inequality in (
216
). In (iv), we leveraged the monotonicity of the trace norm under partial traces. Finally, in (v), we exploited (
202
). This concludes the proof of the second limit in (
205
).

Now, we only need to prove the third limit in (
205
):

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}D_{3/2}(\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\|\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)})

\displaystyle=H_{1/2}(A|B)_{\Psi}\,.

(221)

Since the definition of conditional Petz-Rényi entropy and the duality relation in Lemma
23
imply that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptD_{3/2}(\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\|\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)})=-H_{3/2}(A|EE^{\prime})_{\Psi^{(k)}}=H_{1/2}(A|B)_{\Psi^{(k)}}\,,

(222)

we only need to show that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}H_{1/2}(A|B)_{\Psi^{(k)}}=H_{1/2}(A|B)_{\Psi}\,,

(223)

which is equivalent to prove that

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AB}^{(k)}}\,\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{B}^{(k)}}\right]=\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AB}}\,\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{B}}\right]\,.

(224)

The proof of this limit is exactly the same as the limit in (
210
), i.e.
\lim\limits_{k\rightarrow\infty}\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\,\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}^{(k)}}\right]=\Tr\left[\sqrt{\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}}\,\mathbb{1}_{A}\otimes\sqrt{\Psi_{EE^{\prime}}}\right]
. Indeed, to prove the latter, we only exploited the fact that
\Psi_{AEE^{\prime}}
is Gaussian and that the convergence
\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}^{(k)}\xrightarrow{k\rightarrow\infty}\Psi_{ABEE^{\prime}}
is exponentially fast in trace norm.
∎

C.2
Two-way quantum capacity and secret-key capacity

In this paragraph, we establish a lower bound on the
n
-shot two-way quantum capacity and one-shot secret-key capacity of Gaussian channels, presented in the forthcoming Theorem
45
.

We start by stating the following result regarding the
one-shot distillable entanglement
and the
one-shot distillable key
of
finite-dimensional
states
[
11
]
. Roughly speaking, the one-shot distillable entanglement of a bipartite quantum state
\rho_{AB}
is the maximum number of ebits that can be distilled from
\rho_{AB}
with an error not exceeding
\varepsilon
by exploiting LOCCs. Analogously, the one-shot distillable key of a bipartite quantum state
\rho_{AB}
is the maximum number of secret-key bits that can be generated from
\rho_{AB}
with an error not exceeding
\varepsilon
by exploiting LOCCs. We refer to
[
11
]
for rigorous definitions of one-shot distillable entanglement and one-shot distillable key.

Lemma 46

((Lower bound on the one-shot distillable entanglement and one-shot distillable key
[
11
]
))
.

Let
\rho_{AB}
be a bipartite quantum state on finite-dimensional systems
A
and
B
. For all
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
\eta\in[0,\sqrt{\varepsilon})
, the one-shot distillable key
E_{K}^{(\varepsilon)}
and the one-shot distillable entanglement
E_{D}^{(\varepsilon)}
of
\rho_{AB}
satisfy the following inequality:

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0ptE^{(\varepsilon)}_{K}(\rho)\geq E^{(\varepsilon)}_{D}(\rho)\geq-H_{\max}^{\sqrt{\varepsilon}-\eta}(A|B)_{\rho}+4\log_{2}\eta\,.

(225)

Thanks to the above lemma, we can easily derive a lower bound on the
n
-shot two-way quantum capacity and
n
-shot secret-key capacity of finite-dimensional quantum channels.

Lemma 47

((Lower bound on the
n
-shot two-way quantum capacity and one-shot secret-key capacity of finite-dimensional channels))
.

Let
\mathcal{N}_{A^{\prime}\to B}
be a quantum channel, where
A^{\prime}
and
B
